The following description:

* The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from U, S, or HS modes.
* Systems should implement carefully considered access control policies from lower privilege modes to physical entropy sources.
* The system can trap attempted access to seed and feed a less privileged client virtual entropy source data (Section 32.4.2.3) instead of invoking an SP 800-90B (Section 32.4.2.1) or PTG.2 (Section 32.4.2.2) physical entropy source.
* Implementations may implement mseccfg such that [s,u]seed is a read-only constant value 0.
* Software may discover if access to the seed CSR can be enabled in U and S mode by writing a 1 to [s,u]seed and reading back the result.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from U, S, or HS modes.
RULE 2: Systems should implement carefully considered access control policies from lower privilege modes to physical entropy sources regarding the mseccfg.
RULE 3: The system can trap attempted access to the seed and feed a less privileged client virtual entropy source data instead of invoking an SP 800-90B or PTG.2 physical entropy source.
RULE 4: Implementations may implement mseccfg such that the [s,u]seed field is a read-only constant value 0.
RULE 5: Software may discover if access to the seed CSR can be enabled in U and S mode by writing a 1 to [s,u]seed and reading back the result.

The following description:

* The direct `mseccfg.[s,u]seed` option allows one to draw a security boundary around a component in relation to Sensitive Security Parameter (SSP) flows, even if that component is not in M mode.
* Such modules [referring to the mechanism enabled by `mseccfg`] can enforce the entire key lifecycle from birth (in the entropy source) to death (zeroization) to occur without the key being passed across the boundary to external code.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The direct `mseccfg.[s,u]seed` option allows one to draw a security boundary around a component in relation to Sensitive Security Parameter (SSP) flows, even if that component is not in M mode.
RULE 2: The mechanism enabled by `mseccfg` can enforce the entire key lifecycle from birth in the entropy source to death zeroization without the key being passed across the boundary to external code.

The following description:

*   The register `mseccfg` is allocated at address 0x747.
    *   Address: 0x747
    *   Privilege Level: MRW
    *   Name: mseccfg
    *   Description: Machine security configuration register.

*   The register `mseccfgh` is allocated at address 0x757.

    *   Privilege Level: MRW
    *   Name: mseccfgh

RULE 2: The privilege level for the `mseccfg` register is MRW.
RULE 3: The `mseccfg` register is defined as the Machine security configuration register.
RULE 4: In RV32 only, the register `mseccfgh` represents the upper 32 bits of `mseccfg`.

The following description:

mseccfg is a 64-bit read/write register that controls security features. It exists if any extension that adds a field to mseccfg is implemented; otherwise, it is reserved.

The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from modes less privileged than M.

When USEED is 0, access to the seed CSR in U-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
When USEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from U-mode is allowed; all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.
If Zkr or U-mode is not implemented, USEED is read-only zero.

When SSEED is 0, access to the seed CSR from S-/HS-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
When SSEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from S-/HS-mode is allowed; all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.
If Zkr or S-mode is not implemented, SSEED is read-only zero.

When the H extension is also implemented, access to the seed CSR from an HS-qualified instruction leads to a virtual-instruction exception in VS and VU modes; all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.

The Smepmp extension adds the RLB, MMWP, and the MML fields in mseccfg.

Regarding mseccfg.RLB (Rule Locking Bypass):
When mseccfg.RLB is 1, locked PMP rules may be removed or modified and locked PMP rules may be edited.
When mseccfg.RLB is 0 and pmpcfg.L is 1 in any rule or entry (including disabled entries), then mseccfg.RLB remains 0 and any further modifications to mseccfg.RLB are ignored until a PMP reset.

Regarding mseccfg.MMWP (Machine-Mode Allowlist Policy):
The mseccfg.MMWP is a WARL field that changes the default PMP policy for Machine mode when accessing memory regions that don't have a matching PMP rule.
When mseccfg.MMWP is set, it changes the default PMP policy for M-mode when accessing memory regions that don't have a matching PMP rule, to denied instead of ignored.
This field is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset.

Regarding mseccfg.MML (Machine Mode Lockdown):
The mseccfg.MML is a WARL field that changes the interpretation of the pmpcfg.L bit defined in Section 3.7.1.2.
This field is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset.
When mseccfg.MML is set, the system's behavior changes in the following way:
1. The meaning of pmpcfg.L changes: Instead of marking a rule as locked and enforced in all modes, it now marks a rule as M-mode-only when set and S/U-mode-only when unset.
2. An M-mode-only rule is enforced on Machine mode and denied in Supervisor or User mode. This rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
3. An S/U-mode-only rule is enforced on Supervisor and User modes and denied on Machine mode.
4. A Shared-Region rule is enforced on all modes, with restrictions depending on the pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.X bits:
    *   A Shared-Region rule where pmpcfg.L is not set can be used for sharing data between M-mode and S/U-mode, so is not executable. M-mode has read/write access to that region, and S/U-mode has read access if pmpcfg.X is not set, or read/write access if pmpcfg.X is set.
    *   A Shared-Region rule where pmpcfg.L is set can be used for sharing code between M-mode and S/U-mode, so is not writable. Both M-mode and S/U-mode have execute access on the region, and M-mode also has read access if pmpcfg.X is set. This rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
    *   The encoding pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 can be used for sharing data between M-mode and S/U-mode, where both modes only have read-only access to the region. This rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
5. Adding a rule with executable privileges that either is M-mode-only or a locked Shared-Region is not possible, and such pmpcfg writes are ignored, leaving pmpcfg unchanged. This restriction can be temporarily lifted by setting mseccfg.RLB, e.g., during the boot process.
6. Executing code with Machine mode privileges is only possible from memory regions with a matching M-mode-only rule or a locked Shared-Region rule with executable privileges. Executing code from a region without a matching rule or with a matching S/U-mode-only rule is denied.
7. If mseccfg.MML is not set, the combination of pmpcfg.RW=01 remains reserved for future standard use.

Regarding the Zicfilp extension:
The Zicfilp extension adds the MLPE field in mseccfg.
When the MLPE field is 1, the Zicfilp extension is enabled in M-mode.
When the MLPE field is 0, the Zicfilp extension is not enabled in M-mode, and the following rules apply to M-mode:
*   The hart does not update the ELP state; it remains as NO_LP_EXPECTED.
*   The LPAD instruction operates as a no-op.

Regarding the register size:
When XLEN=32 only, mseccfgh is a 32-bit read/write register that aliases bits 63:32 of mseccfg.
Register mseccfgh exists when XLEN=32 and mseccfg is implemented; it does not exist when XLEN=64.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: mseccfg is a 64-bit read/write register that controls security features.
RULE 2: mseccfg exists if any extension that adds a field to mseccfg is implemented; otherwise, it is reserved.
RULE 3: The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from modes less privileged than M.
RULE 4: When USEED is 0, access to the seed CSR in U-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 5: When USEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from U-mode is allowed, and all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 6: If Zkr or U-mode is not implemented, the USEED field in mseccfg is read-only zero.
RULE 7: When SSEED is 0, access to the seed CSR from S-/HS-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 8: When SSEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from S-/HS-mode is allowed, and all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 9: If Zkr or S-mode is not implemented, the SSEED field in mseccfg is read-only zero.
RULE 10: When the H extension is implemented, access to the seed CSR from an HS-qualified instruction leads to a virtual-instruction exception in VS and VU modes, and all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 11: The Smepmp extension adds the RLB, MMWP, and the MML fields in mseccfg.
RULE 12: When mseccfg.RLB is 1, locked PMP rules may be removed or modified and locked PMP rules may be edited.
RULE 13: When mseccfg.RLB is 0 and pmpcfg.L is 1 in any rule or entry, then mseccfg.RLB remains 0 and any further modifications to mseccfg.RLB are ignored until a PMP reset.
RULE 14: The mseccfg.MMWP is a WARL field that changes the default PMP policy for Machine mode when accessing memory regions that don't have a matching PMP rule.
RULE 15: When mseccfg.MMWP is set, it changes the default PMP policy for M-mode when accessing memory regions that don't have a matching PMP rule, to denied instead of ignored.
RULE 16: The mseccfg.MMWP is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset.
RULE 17: The mseccfg.MML is a WARL field that changes the interpretation of the pmpcfg.L bit defined in Section 3.7.1.2.
RULE 18: The mseccfg.MML is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset.
RULE 19: When mseccfg.MML is set, the meaning of pmpcfg.L changes, marking a rule as M-mode-only when set and S/U-mode-only when unset.
RULE 20: When mseccfg.MML is set, an M-mode-only rule is enforced on Machine mode and denied in Supervisor or User mode, and this rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
RULE 21: When mseccfg.MML is set, an S/U-mode-only rule is enforced on Supervisor and User modes and denied on Machine mode.
RULE 22: When mseccfg.MML is set, a Shared-Region rule is enforced on all modes, with restrictions depending on the pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.X bits.
RULE 23: A Shared-Region rule where pmpcfg.L is not set can be used for sharing data between M-mode and S/U-mode, where it is not executable and M-mode has read/write access to that region, and S/U-mode has read access if pmpcfg.X is not set, or read/write access if pmpcfg.X is set.
RULE 24: A Shared-Region rule where pmpcfg.L is set can be used for sharing code between M-mode and S/U-mode, where it is not writable, both M-mode and S/U-mode have execute access on the region, and M-mode also has read access if pmpcfg.X is set, and this rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
RULE 25: The encoding pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 can be used for sharing data between M-mode and S/U-mode, where both modes only have read-only access to the region, and this rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
RULE 26: Adding a rule with executable privileges that either is M-mode-only or a locked Shared-Region is not possible, and such pmpcfg writes are ignored, leaving pmpcfg unchanged, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
RULE 27: Executing code with Machine mode privileges is only possible from memory regions with a matching M-mode-only rule or a locked Shared-Region rule with executable privileges.
RULE 28: Executing code from a region without a matching rule or with a matching S/U-mode-only rule is denied.
RULE 29: If mseccfg.MML is not set, the combination of pmpcfg.RW=01 remains reserved for future standard use.
RULE 30: The Zicfilp extension adds the MLPE field in mseccfg.
RULE 31: When the MLPE field is 1 in mseccfg, the Zicfilp extension is enabled in M-mode.
RULE 32: When the MLPE field is 0 in mseccfg, the Zicfilp extension is not enabled in M-mode.
RULE 33: When the MLPE field is 0 in mseccfg, the hart does not update the ELP state and it remains as NO_LP_EXPECTED.
RULE 34: When the MLPE field is 0 in mseccfg, the LPAD instruction operates as a no-op.
RULE 35: When XLEN is 32, mseccfgh is a 32-bit read/write register that aliases bits 63:32 of mseccfg.
RULE 36: The register mseccfgh exists when XLEN is 32 and mseccfg is implemented, but it does not exist when XLEN is 64.

The following description:

*   The `mseccfg` register is a CSR (Control and Status Register) that introduces the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields, and their associated rules.
*   The physical memory protection rules when `mseccfg.MML` is set to 1 are summarized in the truth table below.

The following rules apply when `mseccfg.MML` is set to 1, based on the combination of bits from the pmpcfg field (corresponding to fields within `mseccfg`):

*   If `mseccfg.L`=0, `mseccfg.R`=0, `mseccfg.W`=0, and `mseccfg.X`=0, the Result is MMode Inaccessible region (Access Exception) and S/U Mode Inaccessible region (Access Exception).
*   If `mseccfg.L`=0, `mseccfg.R`=0, `mseccfg.W`=0, and `mseccfg.X`=1, the Result is MMode Access Exception and S/U Mode Execute-only region.
*   If `mseccfg.L`=0, `mseccfg.R`=0, `mseccfg.W`=1, and `mseccfg.X`=0, the Result is Shared data region: Read/write on Mmode, read-only on S/U mode.
*   If `mseccfg.L`=0, `mseccfg.R`=0, `mseccfg.W`=1, and `mseccfg.X`=1, the Result is Shared data region: Read/write for both Mand S/U mode.
*   If `mseccfg.L`=0, `mseccfg.R`=1, `mseccfg.W`=0, and `mseccfg.X`=0, the Result is Read-only region.
*   If `mseccfg.L`=0, `mseccfg.R`=1, `mseccfg.W`=0, and `mseccfg.X`=1, the Result is Read/Execute region.
*   If `mseccfg.L`=0, `mseccfg.R`=1, `mseccfg.W`=1, and `mseccfg.X`=0, the Result is Read/Write region.
*   If `mseccfg.L`=0, `mseccfg.R`=1, `mseccfg.W`=1, and `mseccfg.X`=1, the Result is Read/Write/Execute region.
*   If `mseccfg.L`=1, `mseccfg.R`=0, `mseccfg.W`=0, and `mseccfg.X`=0, the Result is Locked inaccessible region* (Access Exception).
*   If `mseccfg.L`=1, `mseccfg.R`=0, `mseccfg.W`=0, and `mseccfg.X`=1, the Result is Locked Execute-only region*.
*   If `mseccfg.L`=1, `mseccfg.R`=0, `mseccfg.W`=1, and `mseccfg.X`=0, the Result is Locked Shared code region: Execute only on both Mand S/U mode.*
*   If `mseccfg.L`=1, `mseccfg.R`=0, `mseccfg.W`=1, and `mseccfg.X`=1, the Result is Locked Shared code region: Execute only on S/U mode, read/execute on Mmode.*
*   If `mseccfg.L`=1, `mseccfg.R`=1, `mseccfg.W`=0, and `mseccfg.X`=0, the Result is Locked Read-only region*.
*   If `mseccfg.L`=1, `mseccfg.R`=1, `mseccfg.W`=0, and `mseccfg.X`=1, the Result is Locked Read/Execute region*.
*   If `mseccfg.L`=1, `mseccfg.R`=1, `mseccfg.W`=1, and `mseccfg.X`=0, the Result is Locked Read/Write region*.
*   If `mseccfg.L`=1, `mseccfg.R`=1, `mseccfg.W`=1, and `mseccfg.X`=1, the Result is Locked Shared data region: Read only on both Mand S/U mode.*
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mseccfg register is a CSR (Control and Status Register) that introduces the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields, and their associated rules.
RULE 2: The physical memory protection rules are summarized when the mseccfg.MML field is set to 1.
RULE 3: The results derived from the mseccfg register depend on the combination of bits from the pmpcfg field corresponding to fields within mseccfg when mseccfg.MML is set to 1.
RULE 4: If mseccfg.L is 0, mseccfg.R is 0, mseccfg.W is 0, and mseccfg.X is 0, the result is the MMode Inaccessible region (Access Exception) and the S/U Mode Inaccessible region (Access Exception).
RULE 5: If mseccfg.L is 0, mseccfg.R is 0, mseccfg.W is 0, and mseccfg.X is 1, the result is the MMode Access Exception and the S/U Mode Execute-only region.
RULE 6: If mseccfg.L is 0, mseccfg.R is 0, mseccfg.W is 1, and mseccfg.X is 0, the result is a Shared data region: Read/write on Mmode, read-only on S/U mode.
RULE 7: If mseccfg.L is 0, mseccfg.R is 0, mseccfg.W is 1, and mseccfg.X is 1, the result is a Shared data region: Read/write for both Mand S/U mode.
RULE 8: If mseccfg.L is 0, mseccfg.R is 1, mseccfg.W is 0, and mseccfg.X is 0, the result is a Read-only region.
RULE 9: If mseccfg.L is 0, mseccfg.R is 1, mseccfg.W is 0, and mseccfg.X is 1, the result is a Read/Execute region.
RULE 10: If mseccfg.L is 0, mseccfg.R is 1, mseccfg.W is 1, and mseccfg.X is 0, the result is a Read/Write region.
RULE 11: If mseccfg.L is 0, mseccfg.R is 1, mseccfg.W is 1, and mseccfg.X is 1, the result is a Read/Write/Execute region.
RULE 12: If mseccfg.L is 1, mseccfg.R is 0, mseccfg.W is 0, and mseccfg.X is 0, the result is a Locked inaccessible region (Access Exception).
RULE 13: If mseccfg.L is 1, mseccfg.R is 0, mseccfg.W is 0, and mseccfg.X is 1, the result is a Locked Execute-only region*.
RULE 14: If mseccfg.L is 1, mseccfg.R is 0, mseccfg.W is 1, and mseccfg.X is 0, the result is a Locked Shared code region: Execute only on both Mand S/U mode.
RULE 15: If mseccfg.L is 1, mseccfg.R is 0, mseccfg.W is 1, and mseccfg.X is 1, the result is a Locked Shared code region: Execute only on S/U mode, read/execute on Mmode.
RULE 16: If mseccfg.L is 1, mseccfg.R is 1, mseccfg.W is 0, and mseccfg.X is 0, the result is a Locked Read-only region*.
RULE 17: If mseccfg.L is 1, mseccfg.R is 1, mseccfg.W is 0, and mseccfg.X is 1, the result is a Locked Read/Execute region*.
RULE 18: If mseccfg.L is 1, mseccfg.R is 1, mseccfg.W is 1, and mseccfg.X is 0, the result is a Locked Read/Write region*.
RULE 19: If mseccfg.L is 1, mseccfg.R is 1, mseccfg.W is 1, and mseccfg.X is 1, the result is a Locked Shared data region: Read only on both Mand S/U mode.

The following description:

*   When the TW (Timeout Wait) bit in mstatus is set and WRS.NTO is executed in any privilege mode other than M mode, and the WRS.NTO instruction does not complete within an implementation-specific bounded time limit, the WRS.NTO instruction will cause an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 3: WRS.NTO is not specified to cause an illegal-instruction exception if executed in Umode when the governing TW bit is 0.

The following description:

*   A vector context status field, VS, is added to mstatus[10:9] and is shadowed in sstatus[10:9]. This field is defined analogously to the floating-point context status field, FS.

*   Attempts to execute any vector instruction, or to access the vector CSRs, raise an illegal-instruction exception when mstatus.VS is set to Off.

*   When mstatus.VS is set to Initial or Clean, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including the vector CSRs, will change mstatus.VS to Dirty.

*   Implementations may also change mstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when there is no change in vector state.

*   If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is 1.

*   If mstatus.VS is not Dirty, mstatus.SD is set in accordance with existing specifications.

*   Implementations may have a writable misa.V field. The mstatus.VS field may exist even if misa.V is clear.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: A vector context status field, VS, is added to mstatus[10:9] and is shadowed in sstatus[10:9].
RULE 2: Attempts to execute any vector instruction or to access the vector CSRs must raise an illegal-instruction exception when mstatus.VS is set to Off.
RULE 3: When mstatus.VS is set to Initial or Clean, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including the vector CSRs, must change mstatus.VS to Dirty.
RULE 4: Implementations may change mstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when there is no change in vector state.
RULE 5: If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD must be 1.
RULE 6: If mstatus.VS is not Dirty, mstatus.SD must be set in accordance with existing specifications.
RULE 7: Implementations may have a writable misa.V field, and the mstatus.VS field may exist even if misa.V is clear.

The following description:

*   When V=1 and either vsstatus.VS or mstatus.VS is set to Off, attempts to execute any vector instruction or to access the vector CSRs raise an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 3: Implementations must be able to change mstatus.VS or vsstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when there is no change in vector state.
RULE 4: If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD must be 1.
RULE 5: If mstatus.VS is not Dirty, mstatus.SD must be set in accordance with existing specifications.

The following description:

The mstatus register is an MXLEN-bit read/write register formatted as shown in Figure 7 for RV32 and Figure 8 for RV64.

The mstatus register keeps track of and controls the hart's current operating state.

A restricted view of mstatus appears as the sstatus register in the S-level ISA.

For RV32 only, mstatush is a 32-bit read/write register formatted as shown in Figure 9.

Bits 30:4 of mstatush generally contain the same fields found in bits 62:36 of mstatus for RV64.

The fields SD, SXL, and UXL do not exist in mstatush.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mstatus register is an MXLEN-bit read/write register formatted as shown in Figure 7 for RV32 and Figure 8 for RV64.
RULE 2: The mstatus register keeps track of and controls the hart's current operating state.
RULE 3: A restricted view of mstatus appears as the sstatus register in the S-level ISA.
RULE 4: For RV32 only, mstatush is a 32-bit read/write register formatted as shown in Figure 9.
RULE 5: Bits 30:4 of mstatush generally contain the same fields found in bits 62:36 of mstatus for RV64.
RULE 6: The fields SD, SXL, and UXL do not exist in mstatus.

The following description:

*   For RV32, the MDT bit is in mstatush and the MIE bit is in the mstatus register.

*   When a trap occurs when executing in M-mode with mnstatus.NMIE set to 0 (or if the Smrnmi extension is implemented and mnstatus.NMIE is 0), the hart enters a critical-error state without updating any architectural state, including the pc. Whether performance counters and timers are updated in the critical-error state is UNSPECIFIED.

*   The MNRET instruction, provided by the Smrnmi extension, sets the MDT bit to 0 if the new privilege mode is not M. If it is U, VS, or VU, then sstatus.SDT is also set to 0. Additionally, if it is VU, then vsstatus.SDT is also set to 0.

RULE 5: If the new privilege mode following the MRET instruction is VU, the vsstatus.SDT bit is also set to 0.

RULE 7: If the new privilege mode following the MNRET instruction is U, VS, or VU, then the sstatus.SDT bit is also set to 0.

The following description:

*   For RV64 harts, the SXL and UXL fields are WARL fields that control the value of XLEN for S-mode and Umode, respectively.
*   The encoding of the SXL and UXL fields is the same as the MXL field of misa, as shown in Table 11.
*   The effective XLEN in S-mode is termed SXLEN.
*   The effective XLEN in U-mode is termed UXLEN.
*   When MXLEN=32, the SXL and UXL fields do not exist, and SXLEN=32 and UXLEN=32.
*   When MXLEN=64, if S-mode is not supported, then SXL is read-only zero.
*   When MXLEN=64, if S-mode is supported, SXL is a WARL field that encodes the current value of SXLEN.
    *   An implementation may make SXL be a read-only field whose value always ensures that SXLEN=MXLEN.
*   When MXLEN=64, if U-mode is not supported, then UXL is read-only zero.
*   When MXLEN=64, if U-mode is supported, UXL is a WARL field that encodes the current value of UXLEN.
    *   An implementation may make UXL be a read-only field whose value always ensures that UXLEN=MXLEN or UXLEN=SXLEN.
*   If S-mode is implemented, the set of legal values that the UXL field may assume excludes those that would cause UXLEN to be greater than SXLEN.
*   Whenever XLEN in any mode is set to a value less than the widest supported XLEN, all operations must ignore source operand register bits above the configured XLEN, and must sign-extend results to fill the entire widest supported XLEN in the destination register.
*   Similarly, pc bits above XLEN are ignored, and when the pc is written, it is sign-extended to fill the widest supported XLEN.
*   Operations must always fill the entire underlying hardware registers with defined values to avoid implementation-defined behavior.
*   The architecture imposes no checks that lower-privilege modes have XLEN settings less than or equal to the next-higher privilege mode.
*   The implementation choice regarding eliding register write-back for some HINTs is observable only by privilege modes with an XLEN setting greater than the current XLEN; it is invisible to the current privilege mode.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: For RV64 harts, the SXL and UXL fields are WARL fields that control the value of XLEN for S-mode and Umode, respectively, within the mstatus register.
RULE 2: The encoding of the SXL and UXL fields within the mstatus register is the same as the MXL field of misa.
RULE 3: The effective XLEN in S-mode is termed SXLEN.
RULE 4: The effective XLEN in U-mode is termed UXLEN.
RULE 5: When MXLEN equals 32, the SXL and UXL fields do not exist, and SXLEN and UXLEN are both 32.
RULE 6: When MXLEN equals 64 and S-mode is not supported, the SXL field within the mstatus register is read-only zero.
RULE 7: When MXLEN equals 64 and S-mode is supported, the SXL field within the mstatus register is a WARL field that encodes the current value of SXLEN.
RULE 8: When MXLEN equals 64 and U-mode is not supported, the UXL field within the mstatus register is read-only zero.
RULE 9: When MXLEN equals 64 and U-mode is supported, the UXL field within the mstatus register is a WARL field that encodes the current value of UXLEN.
RULE 10: If S-mode is implemented, the set of legal values that the UXL field may assume excludes those that would cause UXLEN to be greater than SXLEN.
RULE 11: Whenever XLEN in any mode is set to a value less than the widest supported XLEN, all operations must ignore source operand register bits above the configured XLEN and must sign-extend results to fill the entire widest supported XLEN in the destination register.
RULE 12: Similarly, pc bits above XLEN are ignored, and when the pc is written, it must be sign-extended to fill the widest supported XLEN.
RULE 13: Operations must always fill the entire underlying hardware registers with defined values to avoid implementation-defined behavior.
RULE 14: The architecture imposes no checks that lower-privilege modes have XLEN settings less than or equal to the next-higher privilege mode.
RULE 15: The implementation choice regarding eliding register write-back for some HINTs is observable only by privilege modes with an XLEN setting greater than the current XLEN and is invisible to the current privilege mode.

The following description:

*   **MPRV (Modify PRiVilege) bit:**
    *   When MPRV=0, explicit memory accesses behave as normal, using the translation and protection mechanisms of the current privilege mode.
    *   When MPRV=1, load and store memory addresses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, as though the current privilege mode were set to MPP.
    *   Instruction address-translation and protection are unaffected by the setting of MPRV.
    *   MPRV is read-only 0 if U-mode is not supported.
    *   An MRET or SRET instruction that changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than M also sets MPRV=0.
    *   When MPRV=1, load and store memory addresses are treated as though the current XLEN were set to MPP's XLEN, following the rules in Section 3.1.6.3.

*   **MXR (Make eXecutable Readable) bit:**
    *   When MXR=0, only loads from pages marked readable (R=1 in Figure 67) will succeed.
    *   When MXR=1, loads from pages marked either readable or executable (R=1 or X=1) will succeed.
    *   MXR has no effect when page-based virtual memory is not in effect.
    *   MXR is read-only 0 if S-mode is not supported.

*   **SUM (permit Supervisor User Memory access) bit:**
    *   When SUM=0, S-mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by Umode (U=1 in Figure 67) will fault.
    *   When SUM=1, these accesses are permitted.
    *   SUM has no effect when page-based virtual memory is not in effect.
    *   SUM is in effect when MPRV=1 and MPP=S.
    *   SUM is read-only 0 if S-mode is not supported or if satp .MODE is read-only 0.

*   **Interaction of MXR and SUM:**
    *   The MXR and SUM mechanisms only affect the interpretation of permissions encoded in page-table entries.
    *   They have no impact on whether access-fault exceptions are raised due to PMAs or PMP.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When the MPRV bit in mstatus is 0, explicit memory accesses behave as normal, using the translation and protection mechanisms of the current privilege mode.
RULE 2: When the MPRV bit in mstatus is 1, load and store memory addresses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, as though the current privilege mode were set to MPP.
RULE 3: The setting of the MPRV bit in mstatus does not affect instruction address-translation and protection.
RULE 4: The MPRV bit in mstatus must be read-only 0 if U-mode is not supported.
RULE 5: An MRET or SRET instruction that changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than M must set the MPRV bit in mstatus to 0.
RULE 6: When the MPRV bit in mstatus is 1, load and store memory addresses are treated as though the current XLEN were set to MPP's XLEN, following the rules in Section 3.1.6.3.
RULE 7: When the MXR bit in mstatus is 0, only loads from pages marked readable (R=1) will succeed.
RULE 8: When the MXR bit in mstatus is 1, loads from pages marked either readable or executable (R=1 or X=1) will succeed.
RULE 9: The MXR bit in mstatus has no effect when page-based virtual memory is not in effect.
RULE 10: The MXR bit in mstatus must be read-only 0 if S-mode is not supported.
RULE 11: When the SUM bit in mstatus is 0, S-mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by Umode (U=1) will fault.
RULE 12: When the SUM bit in mstatus is 1, S-mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by Umode are permitted.
RULE 13: The SUM bit in mstatus has no effect when page-based virtual memory is not in effect.
RULE 14: The SUM bit in mstatus is in effect when the MPRV bit is 1 and MPP is S.
RULE 15: The SUM bit in mstatus must be read-only 0 if S-mode is not supported or if satp .MODE is read-only 0.
RULE 16: The MXR and SUM mechanisms only affect the interpretation of permissions encoded in page-table entries.
RULE 17: The MXR and SUM mechanisms have no impact on whether access-fault exceptions are raised due to PMAs or PMP.

The following description:

*   The MBE, SBE, and UBE bits in mstatus and mstatush are WARL fields that control the endianness of memory accesses other than instruction fetches.
*   Instruction fetches are always little-endian.
*   MBE controls whether non-instruction-fetch memory accesses made from M-mode are little-endian (MBE=0) or big-endian (MBE=1).
*   If S-mode is not supported, SBE is read-only 0.
*   If S-mode is supported, SBE controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from S-mode are little-endian (SBE=0) or big-endian (SBE=1).
*   If U-mode is not supported, UBE is read-only 0.
*   If U-mode is supported, UBE controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from U-mode are little-endian (UBE=0) or big-endian (UBE=1).
*   For implicit accesses to supervisor-level memory management data structures, endianness is always controlled by SBE.
*   If any supervisor-level memory management data structures remain in use across a change to SBE, M-mode software must follow such a change to SBE by executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1 = x0 and rs2 = x0.
*   If S-mode is supported, an implementation may make SBE be a read-only copy of MBE.
*   If U-mode is supported, an implementation may make UBE be a read-only copy of either MBE or SBE.
*   An implementation supports only little-endian memory accesses if fields MBE, SBE, and UBE are all read-only 0.
*   An implementation supports only big-endian memory accesses (aside from instruction fetches) if MBE is read-only 1 and SBE and UBE are each read-only 1 when S-mode and U-mode are supported.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The MBE, SBE, and UBE bits in mstatus and mstatush are WARL fields that control the endianness of memory accesses other than instruction fetches.
RULE 2: Instruction fetches are always little-endian.
RULE 3: MBE controls whether non-instruction-fetch memory accesses made from M-mode are little-endian when MBE is 0 or big-endian when MBE is 1.
RULE 4: If S-mode is not supported, SBE is read-only 0.
RULE 5: If S-mode is supported, SBE controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from S-mode are little-endian when SBE is 0 or big-endian when SBE is 1.
RULE 6: If U-mode is not supported, UBE is read-only 0.
RULE 7: If U-mode is supported, UBE controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from U-mode are little-endian when UBE is 0 or big-endian when UBE is 1.
RULE 8: Endianness for implicit accesses to supervisor-level memory management data structures is always controlled by SBE.
RULE 9: If any supervisor-level memory management data structures remain in use across a change to SBE, M-mode software must follow such a change to SBE by executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1 = x0 and rs2 = x0.
RULE 10: If S-mode is supported, an implementation may make SBE be a read-only copy of MBE.
RULE 11: If U-mode is supported, an implementation may make UBE be a read-only copy of either MBE or SBE.
RULE 12: An implementation supports only little-endian memory accesses if fields MBE, SBE, and UBE are all read-only 0.
RULE 13: An implementation supports only big-endian memory accesses (aside from instruction fetches) if MBE is read-only 1 and SBE and UBE are each read-only 1 when S-mode and U-mode are supported.

The following description:

* Machine and Supervisor modes share a single copy of the FS, VS, and XS bits.
* Supervisor-level software normally uses the FS, VS, and XS bits directly to record the status with respect to the supervisor-level saved context.
* Machine-level software must be more conservative in saving and restoring the extension state in their corresponding version of the context.
* The SD bit is read-only and is set when either the FS, VS, or XS bits encode a Dirty state (i.e., SD=(FS==0b11 OR XS==0b11 OR VS==0b11)). This allows privileged code to quickly determine when no additional context save is required beyond the integer register set and pc.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The FS, VS, and XS bits are shared across both Machine and Supervisor modes within the mstatus register.
RULE 2: Supervisor-level software normally uses the FS, VS, and XS bits directly to record the status with respect to the supervisor-level saved context in the mstatus register.
RULE 3: Machine-level software must be more conservative in saving and restoring the extension state in their corresponding version of the context managed by mstatus.
RULE 4: The SD bit is read-only within mstatus.
RULE 5: The SD bit is set when the FS, VS, or XS bits encode a Dirty state, specifically when SD equals 1 if FS equals 0b11, or XS equals 0b11, or VS equals 0b11.
RULE 6: The setting of the SD bit allows privileged code to quickly determine when no additional context save is required beyond the integer register set and pc.

The following description:

*   The Wait for Interrupt (WFI) instruction may raise an illegal-instruction exception when TW=1 in mstatus.
*   The operation of WFI must be unaffected by the global interrupt bits in mstatus (MIE and SIE).
*   The hart must resume if a locally enabled interrupt becomes pending, even if it has been delegated to a less privileged mode, as governed by the delegation register mideleg.
*   WFI should honor the individual interrupt enables (e.g., MTIE); implementations should avoid resuming the hart if the interrupt is pending but not individually enabled.

RULE 3: The hart must resume if a locally enabled interrupt becomes pending, even if that interrupt has been delegated to a less privileged mode as governed by the delegation register mideleg.
RULE 4: The Wait for Interrupt instruction should honor individual interrupt enables, such as MTIE.
RULE 5: Implementations should avoid resuming the hart if an interrupt is pending but not individually enabled.
RULE 6: The Wait for Interrupt instruction is required to resume execution for locally enabled interrupts pending at any privilege level, regardless of the global interrupt enable at each privilege level.

The following description:

*   Access to the v registers of the vector extension is blocked when the VS field is initialized to zero, either at machine level or by the OS itself initializing sstatus.
*   The existence of the FS and VS fields in the sstatus register is an exception to the potential covert channel creation between the f registers of the RISC-V floating-point extensions and the v registers of the vector extension.
*   One way to prevent the use of new user-level CSRs as covert channels would be to add to mstatus or sstatus an "XS" field for each relevant extension, paralleling the V extension's VS field.
*   The "enable" flags that are needed to plug covert channels are not generally expected to require swapping on context switches of user threads, making them a less-than-compelling candidate for inclusion in sstatus.
*   A new place is provided for the "enable" flags instead of including them in sstatus.
*   There is no need to strain sstatus (and add sstatush) for the purpose of plugging covert channels.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: Access to the v registers of the vector extension is blocked when the VS field in sstatus is initialized to zero, whether that initialization occurs at the machine level or by the OS initializing sstatus.
RULE 2: The existence of the FS and VS fields in the sstatus register serves as an exception to the potential covert channel creation between the f registers of the RISC-V floating-point extensions and the v registers of the vector extension.
RULE 3: One method to prevent the use of new user-level CSRs as covert channels is to add an "XS" field to mstatus or sstatus for each relevant extension, paralleling the V extension's VS field.
RULE 4: The "enable" flags required to plug covert channels are not generally expected to require swapping on context switches of user threads, which makes them a less-than-compelling candidate for inclusion in sstatus.
RULE 5: A new location is provided for the "enable" flags instead of including them in sstatus.
RULE 6: There is no need to strain sstatus or add sstatush for the purpose of plugging covert channels.

The following description:

*   The mnstatus CSR holds a two-bit field, MNPP, which on entry to the RNMI trap handler holds the privilege mode of the interrupted context, encoded in the same manner as mstatus.MPP.
*   The mnstatus CSR holds a one-bit field, MNPV, which on entry to the RNMI trap handler holds the virtualization mode of the interrupted context, encoded in the same manner as mstatus.MPV.
*   If the Zicfilp extension is implemented, mnstatus also holds the MNPELP field, which on entry to the RNMI trap handler holds the previous ELP state.
*   When an RNMI trap is taken, MNPELP is set to ELP and ELP is set to 0.
*   mnstatus also holds the NMIE bit.
*   When NMIE=1, non-maskable interrupts are enabled.
*   When NMIE=0, all interrupts are disabled.
*   When NMIE=0, the hart behaves as though mstatus.MPRV were clear, regardless of the current setting of mstatus.MPRV.
*   Upon reset, NMIE contains the value 0.
*   The other bits in mnstatus are reserved; software should write zeros and hardware implementations should return zeros.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mnstatus CSR holds a two-bit field, MNPP, which on entry to the RNMI trap handler holds the privilege mode of the interrupted context, encoded in the same manner as mstatus.MPP.
RULE 2: The mnstatus CSR holds a one-bit field, MNPV, which on entry to the RNMI trap handler holds the virtualization mode of the interrupted context, encoded in the same manner as mstatus.MPV.
RULE 3: If the Zicfilp extension is implemented, mnstatus also holds the MNPELP field, which on entry to the RNMI trap handler holds the previous ELP state.
RULE 4: When an RNMI trap is taken, the MNPELP field is set to ELP and the ELP field is set to 0.
RULE 5: The mnstatus CSR also holds the NMIE bit.
RULE 6: When the NMIE bit is 1, non-maskable interrupts are enabled.
RULE 7: When the NMIE bit is 0, all interrupts are disabled.
RULE 8: When the NMIE bit is 0, the hart behaves as though mstatus.MPRV were clear, regardless of the current setting of mstatus.MPRV.
RULE 9: Upon reset, the NMIE bit in mnstatus contains the value 0.
RULE 10: The other bits in mnstatus are reserved, and software should write zeros while hardware implementations should return zeros.

The following description:

*   An attempt to execute SINVAL.VMA or HINVAL.GVMA in S-mode or HS-mode when mstatus .TVM=1 also raises an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 4: SFENCE.W.INVAL and SFENCE.INVAL.IR instructions do not need to be trapped when mstatus.TVM is 1 or when hstatus.VTVM is 1 because they only have ordering effects but no visible side effects.

The following description:

The facilities provided by the hstatus register are analogous to the mstatus register for tracking and controlling the exception behavior of a VS-mode guest.

Regarding exception handling behavior related to VS-mode instructions:

*   When the VSXL field controls the effective XLEN for VS-mode (known as VSXLEN):
    *   When HSXLEN=32, the VSXL field does not exist, and VSXLEN=32.
    *   When HSXLEN=64, VSXL is a WARL field that is encoded the same as the MXL field of misa (Table 11).
    *   An implementation may make VSXL be a read-only field whose value always ensures that VSXLEN=HSXLEN.
    *   If HSXLEN is changed from 32 to a wider width, and if field VSXL is not restricted to a single value, it gets the value corresponding to the widest supported width not wider than the new HSXLEN.

Regarding the fields VTSR, VTW, and VTVM, which are defined analogously to the mstatus fields TSR, TW, and TVM:

*   When VTSR=1: An attempt in VS-mode to execute SRET raises a virtual-instruction exception.
*   When VTW=1 (and assuming mstatus .TW=0): An attempt in VS-mode to execute WFI raises a virtual-instruction exception if the WFI does not complete within an implementation-specific, bounded time limit.
*   When VTW=1 (and mstatus .TW=0): An implementation may have WFI always raise a virtual-instruction exception in VS-mode, even if there are pending globally-disabled interrupts when the instruction is executed.
*   When VTVM=1: An attempt in VS-mode to execute SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA or to access CSR satp raises a virtual-instruction exception.

Regarding the VSBE bit:

*   The VSBE bit is a WARL field that controls the endianness of explicit memory accesses made from VS-mode.
    *   If VSBE=0: Explicit load and store memory accesses made from VS-mode are little-endian.
    *   If VSBE=1: Explicit load and store memory accesses made from VS-mode are big-endian.
*   An implementation may make VSBE a read-only field that always specifies the same endianness as HS-mode.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When HSXLEN is 32, the VSXL field does not exist, and VSXLEN is 32.
RULE 2: When HSXLEN is 64, the VSXL field is a WARL field that is encoded the same as the MXL field of misa.
RULE 3: When HSXLEN is changed from 32 to a wider width and the VSXL field is not restricted to a single value, it gets the value corresponding to the widest supported width not wider than the new HSXLEN.
RULE 4: When VTSR is 1, an attempt in VS-mode to execute SRET raises a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 5: When VTW is 1 and mstatus.TW is 0, an attempt in VS-mode to execute WFI raises a virtual-instruction exception if the WFI does not complete within an implementation-specific, bounded time limit.
RULE 6: When VTVM is 1, an attempt in VS-mode to execute SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA or to access CSR satp raises a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 7: When VTW is 1 and mstatus.TW is 0, an implementation may have WFI always raise a virtual-instruction exception in VS-mode, even if there are pending globally-disabled interrupts when the instruction is executed.
RULE 8: The VSBE bit is a WARL field that controls the endianness of explicit memory accesses made from VS-mode.
RULE 9: If VSBE is 0, explicit load and store memory accesses made from VS-mode are little-endian.
RULE 10: If VSBE is 1, explicit load and store memory accesses made from VS-mode are big-endian.
RULE 11: An implementation may make the VSBE field a read-only field that always specifies the same endianness as HS-mode.
======================================================= END - 37 =======================================================

The following description:

The hypervisor extension adds two fields, MPV and GVA, to the machine-level mstatus or mstatush CSR, and modifies the behavior of several existing mstatus fields.

When MXLEN=64, Figure 111 shows the modified mstatus register when the hypervisor extension is implemented.

When MXLEN=32, the hypervisor extension adds MPV and GVA not to mstatus but to mstatush.

The MPV bit (Machine Previous Virtualization Mode) is written by the implementation whenever a trap is taken into M-mode.
*   The MPV bit is set to the value of the virtualization mode V at the time of the trap.
*   When an MRET instruction is executed, the virtualization mode V is set to MPV, unless MPP=3, in which case V remains 0.

Field GVA (Guest Virtual Address) is written by the implementation whenever a trap is taken into M-mode.
*   For any trap (breakpoint, address misaligned, access fault, page fault, or guest-page fault) that writes a guest virtual address to mtval, GVA is set to 1.
*   For any other trap into M-mode, GVA is set to 0.

The TSR and TVM fields of mstatus affect execution only in HS-mode, not in VS-mode.

The TW field affects execution in all modes except M-mode.

Setting TVM=1 prevents HS-mode from accessing hgatp or executing HFENCE.GVMA or HINVAL.GVMA.

Setting TVM=1 has no effect on accesses to vsatp or instructions HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA.

Setting TVM=1 does not cause traps for accesses to vsatp or instructions HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA, or for any actions taken in VS-mode.

The hypervisor extension changes the behavior of the Modify Privilege field, MPRV, of mstatus.

When MPRV=0, translation and protection behave as normal.

When MPRV=1, explicit memory accesses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, as though the current virtualization mode were set to MPV and the current nominal privilege mode were set to MPP.

Table 49 enumerates the cases for the effect of MPRV on the translation and protection of explicit memory accesses:

| MPRV | MPV | MPP | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | - | - | Normal access; current privilege mode applies. |
| 1 | 0 | 0 | U-level access with HS-level translation and protection only. |
| 1 | 0 | 1 | HS-level access with HS-level translation and protection only. |
| 1 | - | 3 | M-level access with no translation. |
| 1 | 1 | 0 | VU-level access with two-stage translation and protection. The HS-levelMXR bit makes any executable page readable. vsstatus .MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage, but only if readable at the guest-physical translation stage. |
| 1 | 1 | 1 | VS-level access with two-stage translation and protection. The HS-levelMXR bit makes any executable page readable. vsstatus .MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage, but only if readable at the guest-physical translation stage. vsstatus .SUM applies instead of the HS- level SUMbit. |

MPRV does not affect the virtual-machine load/store instructions, HLV, HLVX, and HSV.

The explicit loads and stores of these instructions always act as though V=1 and the nominal privilege mode were hstatus .SPVP, overriding MPRV.

The mstatus register is a superset of the HS-level sstatus register but is not a superset of vsstatus.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The hypervisor extension adds the fields MPV and GVA to the machine-level mstatus or mstatush CSR and modifies the behavior of several existing mstatus fields.
RULE 2: When the machine extension length is 64, the mstatus register is modified when the hypervisor extension is implemented.
RULE 3: When the machine extension length is 32, the hypervisor extension adds the MPV and GVA fields to mstatush instead of mstatus.
RULE 4: The MPV bit is written by the implementation whenever a trap is taken into M-mode.
RULE 5: When the MPV bit is written, it is set to the value of the virtualization mode V at the time of the trap.
RULE 6: When an MRET instruction is executed, the virtualization mode V is set to MPV, unless the nominal privilege mode MPP is 3, in which case V remains 0.
RULE 7: The GVA field is written by the implementation whenever a trap is taken into M-mode.
RULE 8: GVA is set to 1 for any trap, including breakpoint, address misaligned, access fault, page fault, or guest-page fault, that writes a guest virtual address to mtval.
RULE 9: GVA is set to 0 for any other trap into M-mode.
RULE 10: The TSR and TVM fields of mstatus affect execution only in HS-mode and not in VS-mode.
RULE 11: The TW field affects execution in all modes except M-mode.
RULE 12: Setting TVM=1 prevents HS-mode from accessing hgatp or executing HFENCE.GVMA or HINVAL.GVMA.
RULE 13: Setting TVM=1 has no effect on accesses to vsatp or instructions HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA.
RULE 14: Setting TVM=1 does not cause traps for accesses to vsatp or instructions HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA, or for any actions taken in VS-mode.
RULE 15: The hypervisor extension changes the behavior of the Modify Privilege field, MPRV, of mstatus.
RULE 16: When MPRV is 0, translation and protection behave as normal.
RULE 17: When MPRV is 1, explicit memory accesses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied as though the current virtualization mode were set to MPV and the current nominal privilege mode were set to MPP.
RULE 18: MPRV does not affect the virtual-machine load/store instructions, HLV, HLVX, and HSV.
RULE 19: The explicit loads and stores of the virtual-machine load/store instructions always act as though V=1 and the nominal privilege mode were hstatus .SPVP, overriding MPRV.
RULE 20: The mstatus register is a superset of the HS-level sstatus register but is not a superset of vsstatus.
======================================================= END - 43 =======================================================

The following description:

*   An instruction is HS-qualified if it would be valid to execute in HS-mode (for some values of the instruction's register operands), assuming fields TSR and TVM of CSR mstatus are both zero.

*   TSR and TVM in mstatus are intended to impact only S-mode (HS-mode), and they are ignored for determining exceptions in VS-mode.

*   A virtual-instruction exception (code 22) is normally raised instead of an illegal-instruction exception when V=1 if the attempted instruction is HS-qualified but is prevented from executing when V=1 either due to insufficient privilege or because the instruction is expressly disabled by a supervisor or hypervisor CSR such as scounteren or hcounteren.

*   A virtual-instruction exception is raised for the following cases in VS-mode or VU-mode:
    *   Attempts to access an implemented non-high-half hypervisor CSR or VS CSR when the same access (read/write) would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus .TVM=0.
    *   If XLEN=32, attempts to access an implemented high-half VS CSR when the same access (read/write) to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus .TVM=0.

*   Attempts to execute WFI in VU-mode raise a virtual-instruction exception when mstatus .TW=0.

*   Attempts to execute a supervisor instruction (SRET or SFENCE) in VU-mode raise a virtual-instruction exception when mstatus .TW=0.

*   Attempts to execute WFI in VS-mode raise a virtual-instruction exception when hstatus .VTW=1 and mstatus .TW=0, unless the instruction completes within an implementation-specific, bounded time.

*   Attempts to execute SRET in VS-mode raise a virtual-instruction exception when hstatus .VTSR=1.

*   Attempts to execute an SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA instruction or to access satp in VS-mode raise a virtual-instruction exception when hstatus .VTVM=1.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: An instruction is HS-qualified if it would be valid to execute in HS-mode for some values of the instruction's register operands, assuming fields TSR and TVM of CSR mstatus are both zero.
RULE 2: TSR and TVM in mstatus are intended to impact only S-mode (HS-mode) and are ignored for determining exceptions in VS-mode.
RULE 3: A virtual-instruction exception is normally raised instead of an illegal-instruction exception when V=1 if the attempted instruction is HS-qualified but is prevented from executing when V=1 either due to insufficient privilege or because the instruction is expressly disabled by a supervisor or hypervisor CSR such as scounteren or hcounteren.
RULE 4: A virtual-instruction exception is raised in VS-mode or VU-mode for attempts to access an implemented non-high-half hypervisor CSR or VS CSR when the same access (read/write) would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus .TVM is zero.
RULE 5: A virtual-instruction exception is raised in VS-mode or VU-mode for attempts to access an implemented high-half VS CSR when XLEN is 32 and the same access (read/write) to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus .TVM is zero.
RULE 6: Attempts to execute WFI in VU-mode raise a virtual-instruction exception when mstatus .TW is zero.
RULE 7: Attempts to execute a supervisor instruction, such as SRET or SFENCE, in VU-mode raise a virtual-instruction exception when mstatus .TW is zero.
RULE 8: Attempts to execute WFI in VS-mode raise a virtual-instruction exception when hstatus .VTW is one and mstatus .TW is zero, unless the instruction completes within an implementation-specific, bounded time.
RULE 9: Attempts to execute SRET in VS-mode raise a virtual-instruction exception when hstatus .VTSR is one.
RULE 10: Attempts to execute an SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA instruction or to access satp in VS-mode raise a virtual-instruction exception when hstatus .VTVM is one.

The following description:

When a trap is taken into M-mode:
*   Virtualization mode V gets set to 0.
*   Fields MPV and MPP in mstatus (or mstatush) are set according to Table 52.
*   Fields GVA, MPIE, and MIE in mstatus / mstatush are written.
*   CSRs mepc, mcause, mtval, mtval2, and mtinst are written.

Upon trap return:
*   MPV is ignored when MPP=3.

Table 52. Value of mstatus / mstatush fields MPV and MPP after a trap into M-mode.
| Previous Mode | MPV | MPP |
|---------------|-----|-----|
| U-mode        | 0   | 0   |
| HS-mode       | 0   | 1   |
| M-mode        | 0   | 3   |
| VU-mode       | 1   | 0   |
| VS-mode       | 1   | 1   |
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When a trap is taken into M-mode, the virtualization mode V field in mstatus must be set to 0.
RULE 2: When a trap is taken into M-mode, the fields MPV and MPP in mstatus or mstatush must be set according to the values specified in Table 52.
RULE 3: When a trap is taken into M-mode from U-mode, the MPV field in mstatus must be set to 0 and the MPP field in mstatus must be set to 0.
RULE 4: When a trap is taken into M-mode from HS-mode, the MPV field in mstatus must be set to 0 and the MPP field in mstatus must be set to 1.
RULE 5: When a trap is taken into M-mode from M-mode, the MPV field in mstatus must be set to 0 and the MPP field in mstatus must be set to 3.
RULE 6: When a trap is taken into M-mode from VU-mode, the MPV field in mstatus must be set to 1 and the MPP field in mstatus must be set to 0.
RULE 7: When a trap is taken into M-mode from VS-mode, the MPV field in mstatus must be set to 1 and the MPP field in mstatus must be set to 1.
RULE 8: When a trap is taken into M-mode, the fields GVA, MPIE, and MIE in mstatus or mstatush must be written.
RULE 9: When a trap is taken into M-mode, the CSRs mepc, mcause, mtval, mtval2, and mtinst must be written.
RULE 10: Upon trap return, the MPV field in mstatus must be ignored when the MPP field in mstatus is equal to 3.

*   When executing the MRET instruction to return from a trap taken into M-mode, MRET first determines what the new privilege mode will be according to the values of MPP and MPV in mstatus or mstatush, as encoded in Table 52.

The following description:

*   The PBMTE bit controls whether the Svpbmt extension is available for use in S-mode and G-stage address translation (i.e., for page tables pointed to by satp or hgatp).
*   When PBMTE=1, Svpbmt is available for S-mode and G-stage address translation.
*   When PBMTE=0, the implementation behaves as though Svpbmt were not implemented.
*   If Svpbmt is not implemented, PBMTE is read-only zero.
*   Furthermore, for implementations with the hypervisor extension, henvcfg .PBMTE is read-only zero if menvcfg .PBMTE is zero.
*   After changing menvcfg .PBMTE, executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1 = x0 and rs2 = x0 suffices to synchronize address-translation caches with respect to the altered interpretation of page-table entries' PBMT fields.
*   The Svade extension requires page-fault exceptions be raised when PTE A/D bits need be set, hence Svade is implemented when ADUE=0.
*   When ADUE=1, hardware updating of PTE A/D bits is enabled during S-mode address translation, and the implementation behaves as though the Svade extension were not implemented for S-mode address translation.
*   When ADUE=0, the implementation behaves as though Svade were implemented for S-mode and G-stage address translation.
*   If Svadu is not implemented, ADUE is read-only zero.
*   Furthermore, for implementations with the hypervisor extension, henvcfg .ADUE is read-only zero if menvcfg .ADUE is zero.
*   After changing menvcfg .ADUE, executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1 = x0 and rs2 = x0 suffices to synchronize address-translation caches with respect to the altered interpretation of page-table entries' A/D bits.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The PBMTE bit controls whether the Svpbmt extension is available for use in S-mode and G-stage address translation for page tables pointed to by satp or hgatp.
RULE 2: When the PBMTE bit is set to 1, Svpbmt is available for S-mode and G-stage address translation for page tables pointed to by satp or hgatp.
RULE 3: When the PBMTE bit is set to 0, the implementation behaves as though Svpbmt were not implemented for page tables pointed to by satp or hgatp.
RULE 4: If Svpbmt is not implemented, the PBMTE bit must be read-only zero.
RULE 5: For implementations with the hypervisor extension, the henvcfg .PBMTE register must be read-only zero if the menvcfg .PBMTE register is zero.
RULE 6: Executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1 = x0 and rs2 = x0 suffices to synchronize address-translation caches with respect to the altered interpretation of page-table entries' PBMT fields when menvcfg .PBMTE is changed.
RULE 7: When the ADUE bit is 0, the implementation behaves as though the Svade extension were implemented for S-mode and G-stage address translation for page tables pointed to by satp or hgatp.
RULE 8: When the ADUE bit is 1, hardware updating of PTE A/D bits is enabled during S-mode address translation, and the implementation behaves as though the Svade extension were not implemented for S-mode address translation for page tables pointed to by satp or hgatp.
RULE 9: The Svade extension requires page-fault exceptions to be raised when PTE A/D bits need to be set, and Svade is implemented when the ADUE bit is 0.
RULE 10: If Svadu is not implemented, the ADUE bit must be read-only zero.
RULE 11: For implementations with the hypervisor extension, the henvcfg .ADUE register must be read-only zero if the menvcfg .ADUE register is zero.
RULE 12: Executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1 = x0 and rs2 = x0 suffices to synchronize address-translation caches with respect to the altered interpretation of page-table entries' A/D bits when menvcfg .ADUE is changed.

The following description:

The satp CSR is an SXLEN-bit read/write register which controls supervisor-mode address translation and protection.

The register holds the following components:
*   The physical page number (PPN) of the root page table, which is defined as its supervisor physical address divided by 4 KiB.
*   An address space identifier (ASID), which facilitates address-translation fences on a per-addressspace basis.
*   The MODE field, which selects the current address-translation scheme.

Storing a PPN in satp, rather than a physical address, supports a physical address space larger than 4 GiB for RV32. The satp .PPN field might not be capable of holding all physical page numbers, and some platform standards might place constraints on the values satp .PPN may assume, such as requiring that all physical page numbers corresponding to main memory be representable.

The satp CSR is considered active when the effective privilege mode is S-mode or U-mode. Executions of the address-translation algorithm may only begin using a given value of satp when satp is active.

Regarding the ASID field:
*   The number of ASID bits is UNSPECIFIED and may be zero.
*   The number of implemented ASID bits, termed ASIDLEN, may be determined by writing one to every bit position in the ASID field, then reading back the value in satp to see which bit positions in the ASID field hold a one.
*   If ASIDLEN > 0, ASID[ASIDLEN-1:0] is writable.
*   The maximal value of ASIDLEN, termed ASIDMAX, is 9 for Sv32 or 16 for Sv39, Sv48, and Sv57.

Regarding the MODE field:
*   The MODE field selects the current address-translation scheme.
*   When MODE=Bare, supervisor virtual addresses are equal to supervisor physical addresses, and there is no additional memory protection beyond the physical memory protection scheme described in Section 3.7.
*   To select MODE=Bare, software must write zero to the remaining fields of satp (bits 30-0 when SXLEN=32, or bits 59-0 when SXLEN=64).
*   Attempting to select MODE=Bare with a nonzero pattern in the remaining fields has an UNSPECIFIED effect on the value that the remaining fields assume and an UNSPECIFIED effect on address translation and protection behavior.
*   When SXLEN=32, the only other valid setting for MODE is Sv32, a paged virtual-memory scheme described in Section 12.3.
*   When SXLEN=64, three paged virtual-memory schemes are defined: Sv39, Sv48, and Sv57, described in Section 12.4, Section 12.5, and Section 12.6, respectively.
*   The remaining MODE settings are reserved for future use and may define different interpretations of the other fields in satp.
*   Implementations are not required to support all MODE settings; if satp is written with an unsupported MODE, the entire write has no effect, and no fields in satp are modified.

Regarding the encoding constraints:
*   When SXLEN=32, the satp encodings corresponding to MODE=Bare and ASID[8:7]=3 are designated for custom use, whereas the encodings corresponding to MODE=Bare and ASID[8:7]≠3 are reserved for future standard use.
*   When SXLEN=64, all satp encodings corresponding to MODE=Bare are reserved for future standard use.
*   Version 1.11 of this standard stated that the remaining fields in satp had no effect when MODE=Bare.
*   Making these fields reserved facilitates future definition of additional translation and protection modes, particularly in RV32, for which all patterns of the existing MODE field have already been allocated.

Ordering and Synchronization Behavior:
*   Translations that began while satp was active are not required to complete or terminate when satp is no longer active, unless an SFENCE.VMA instruction matching the address and ASID is executed.
*   The SFENCE.VMA instruction must be used to ensure that updates to the addresstranslation data structures are observed by subsequent implicit reads to those structures by a hart.
*   Writing satp does not imply any ordering constraints between page-table updates and subsequent address translations, nor does it imply any invalidation of address-translation caches.
*   If the new address space's page tables have been modified, or if an ASID is reused, it may be necessary to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction (see Section 12.2.1) after, or in some cases before, writing satp.
*   Not imposing upon implementations to flush address-translation caches upon satp writes reduces the cost of context switches, provided a sufficiently large ASID space.

Table 40. Encoding of satp MODE field.

| SXLEN=32 | SXLEN=32 | SXLEN=32 |
|---|---|---|
| Value | Name | Description |
| 0 1 | Bare Sv32 | No translation or protection. Page-based 32-bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.3). |

| SXLEN=64 | SXLEN=64 | SXLEN=64 |
|---|---|---|
| Value | Name | Description |
| 0 | Bare | No translation or protection. |
| 1-7 | - | Reserved for standard use |
| 8 | Sv39 | Page-based 39-bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.4). |
| 9 | Sv48 | Page-based 48-bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.5). |
| 10 | Sv57 | Page-based 57-bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.6). |
| 11 | Sv64 | Reserved for page-based 64-bit virtual addressing. |
| 12-13 | - | Reserved for standard use |
| 14-15 | - | Designated for custom use |
============================================================

RULE 22: When SXLEN=64, all satp encodings corresponding to MODE=Bare are reserved for future standard use.

RULE 24: Translations that began while satp was active are not required to complete or terminate when satp is no longer active, unless an SFENCE.VMA instruction matching the address and ASID is executed.

The following description:

*   Implementations must only perform implicit reads of the translation data structures pointed to by the current contents of the satp register or a subsequent valid (V=1) translation data structure entry.
*   Implementations must only raise exceptions for implicit accesses that are generated as a result of instruction execution, not those that are performed speculatively.
*   Changes to the sstatus fields SUM and MXR take effect immediately, without the need to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction.
*   Changing satp .MODE from Bare to other modes and vice versa takes effect immediately, without the need to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction.
*   Changes to satp .ASID take effect immediately.
*   The meaning of an ASID is local to a hart; software may choose to use the same ASID to refer to different address spaces on different harts.
*   For implementations that make satp .MODE read-only zero (always Bare), attempts to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction might raise an illegal-instruction exception.
*   The property that any translation since the last subsuming SFENCE.VMA might be used applies even if the virtual-address width for that translation differs from the width currently specified by satp.MODE.
*   For a given virtual address and ASID, any translation since the last subsuming SFENCE.VMA might be used, even if that translation used a virtual address of a different width.
*   For a given virtual address, any global translation since the last subsuming SFENCE.VMA might be used, regardless of both ASID and virtual-address width.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: Implementations must only perform implicit reads of the translation data structures pointed to by the current contents of the satp register or a subsequent valid (V=1) translation data structure entry.
RULE 2: Implementations must only raise exceptions for implicit accesses that are generated as a result of instruction execution, not those that are performed speculatively.
RULE 3: Changes to the sstatus fields SUM and MXR take effect immediately without the need to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction.
RULE 4: Changing satp .MODE from Bare to other modes and vice versa takes effect immediately without the need to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction.
RULE 5: Changes to satp .ASID take effect immediately.
RULE 6: The meaning of an ASID is local to a hart, and software may choose to use the same ASID to refer to different address spaces on different harts.
RULE 7: For implementations that make satp .MODE read-only zero (always Bare), attempts to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction might raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 8: The property that any translation since the last subsuming SFENCE.VMA might be used applies even if the virtual-address width for that translation differs from the width currently specified by satp.MODE.
RULE 9: For a given virtual address and ASID, any translation since the last subsuming SFENCE.VMA might be used, even if that translation used a virtual address of a different width.
RULE 10: For a given virtual address, any global translation since the last subsuming SFENCE.VMA might be used, regardless of both ASID and virtual-address width.

RULE 4: The range of addresses that an implementation's page-table walker will touch is fully under supervisor control because the algorithm may only touch page tables reachable from the root page table indicated in satp.

The following description:

*   When V=1, instructions that normally read or modify satp actually access vsatp instead.
*   vsatp controls VS-stage address translation, which is the first stage of two-stage translation for guest virtual addresses.
*   When V=0, a write to vsatp with an unsupported MODE value is either ignored as it is for satp, or the fields of vsatp are treated as WARL in the normal way.
*   When V=1, a write to satp with an unsupported MODE value is ignored and no write to vsatp is effected.
*   When V=0, vsatp does not directly affect the behavior of the machine, unless a virtual-machine load/store (HLV, HLVX, or HSV) or the MPRV feature in the mstatus register is used to execute a load or store as though V=1.

=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When V=1, instructions that normally read or modify satp actually access vsatp instead.
RULE 2: When V=1, a write to satp with an unsupported MODE value is ignored and no write to vsatp is effected.
RULE 3: When V=0, a write to vsatp with an unsupported MODE value is either ignored as it is for satp, or the fields of vsatp are treated as WARL in the normal way.
RULE 4: When V=0, vsatp does not directly affect the behavior of the machine, unless a virtual-machine load/store (HLV, HLVX, or HSV) or the MPRV feature in the mstatus register is used to execute a load or store as though V=1.
RULE 5: vsatp controls VS-stage address translation, which is the first stage of two-stage translation for guest virtual addresses.

============================================================

RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The instruction traps enabled by TVM=1 permit machine level to co-opt both satp and hgatp and substitute shadow page tables that merge the OS's chosen page translations with M-level's lower-stage translations without the OS being aware.
RULE 2: Setting TVM=1 does not cause traps for accesses to vsatp or instructions HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA.
RULE 3: Setting TVM=1 does not cause traps for any actions taken in VS-mode because M-level software is not expected to need to involve itself in VS-stage address translation regarding satp.

The following description:

*   No mechanism is provided to atomically change `vsatp` and `hgatp` together.

*   To prevent speculative execution causing one guest's VS-stage translations to be cached under another guest's VMID, world-switch code should perform the following sequence:
    *   Zero `vsatp`.
    *   Swap `hgatp`.
    *   Finally, write the new `vsatp` value.

*   If `henvcfg .PBMTE/ADUE` need to be world-switched, they should be switched after zeroing `vsatp` but before writing the new `vsatp` value, which obviates the need to execute an `HFENCE.VVMA` instruction.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================

RULE 2: World-switch code should zero `vsatp` to prevent speculative execution causing one guest's VS-stage translations to be cached under another guest's VMID.
RULE 3: World-switch code should swap `hgatp` after zeroing `vsatp`.
RULE 4: World-switch code should write the new `vsatp` value finally.
RULE 5: If `henvcfg .PBMTE/ADUE` need to be world-switched, they should be switched after zeroing `vsatp` but before writing the new `vsatp` value.

The following description:

*   If `satp.MODE` (or `vsatp.MODE` when V=1) is set to Bare and the effective privilege mode is less than M, shadow stack instructions raise a store/AMO access-fault exception.

*   Shadow stack instructions require the G-stage page table to have read-write permission for the accessed memory when G-stage page tables are active; otherwise, a store/AMO guest-page-fault exception is raised.

*   The xwr == 010b encoding in the G-stage PTE remains reserved.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========

RULE 2: Shadow stack instructions require the G-stage page table to have read-write permission for the accessed memory when G-stage page tables are active.
RULE 3: If the G-stage page table does not have read-write permission for the accessed memory when G-stage page tables are active, a store/AMO guest-page-fault exception must be raised for shadow stack instructions.
RULE 4: The xwr == 010b encoding in the G-stage PTE remains reserved.

The following description:

The counter-enable (scounteren) CSR is a 32-bit register that controls the availability of the hardware performance monitoring counters to U-mode.

*   When the CY, TM, IR, or HPMn bit in the scounteren register is clear, attempts to read the cycle, time, instret, or hpmcountern register while executing in U-mode will cause an illegal-instruction exception.
*   When one of the CY, TM, IR, or HPMn bits in the scounteren register is set, access to the corresponding register is permitted.
*   scounteren must be implemented.
*   Any of the bits in scounteren may be read-only zero, which indicates that reads to the corresponding counter will cause an exception when executing in U-mode. These bits are effectively WARL fields.
*   U-mode may only access a counter if the corresponding bits in scounteren and mcounteren are both set.
*   The setting of a bit in mcounteren does not affect whether the corresponding bit in scounteren is writable.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: scounteren is a 32-bit CSR that controls the availability of hardware performance monitoring counters to U-mode.
RULE 2: When the CY, TM, IR, or HPMn bit in the scounteren register is clear, attempting to read the cycle, time, instret, or hpmcountern register while executing in U-mode will cause an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 3: When one of the CY, TM, IR, or HPMn bits in the scounteren register is set, access to the corresponding register is permitted.
RULE 4: scounteren must be implemented.
RULE 5: Any of the bits in scounteren may be read-only zero, which indicates that reads to the corresponding counter will cause an exception when executing in U-mode, and these bits are effectively WARL fields.
RULE 6: U-mode may only access a counter if the corresponding bits in both scounteren and mcounteren are set.
RULE 7: The setting of a bit in mcounteren does not affect whether the corresponding bit in scounteren is writable.

The following description:

*   When V=1, an instruction is prevented from executing due to insufficient privilege or because the instruction is expressly disabled by a supervisor or hypervisor CSR such as `scounteren` or `hcounteren`, resulting in a virtual-instruction exception (code 22) being normally raised instead of an illegal-instruction exception.

*   In VS-mode, attempts to access a non-high-half counter CSR result in a virtual-instruction exception if the corresponding bit in `hcounteren` is 0 and the same bit in `mcounteren` is 1.

*   In VS-mode, if XLEN=32, attempts to access a high-half counter CSR result in a virtual-instruction exception if the corresponding bit in `hcounteren` is 0 and the same bit in `mcounteren` is 1.

*   In VU-mode, attempts to access a non-high-half counter CSR result in a virtual-instruction exception if the corresponding bit in either `hcounteren` or `scounteren` is 0 and the same bit in `mcounteren` is 1.

*   In VU-mode, if XLEN=32, attempts to access a high-half counter CSR result in a virtual-instruction exception if the corresponding bit in either `hcounteren` or `scounteren` is 0 and the same bit in `mcounteren` is 1.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When V=1, an instruction is prevented from executing due to insufficient privilege or because the instruction is expressly disabled by a supervisor or hypervisor CSR such as `scounteren` or `hcounteren`, resulting in a virtual-instruction exception (code 22) being normally raised instead of an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 2: In VU-mode, attempts to access a non-high-half counter CSR result in a virtual-instruction exception if the corresponding bit in either `hcounteren` or `scounteren` is 0 and the same bit in `mcounteren` is 1.
RULE 3: In VU-mode, if XLEN=32, attempts to access a high-half counter counter CSR result in a virtual-instruction exception if the corresponding bit in either `hcounteren` or `scounteren` is 0 and the same bit in `mcounteren` is 1.
